### 5.2.       Les échanges fonciers temporaires : un levier d’action possible mais encore inexploré

L’un des objectifs de cette étude était aussi d’explorer dans une démarche prospective la place actuelle et potentielle des échanges fonciers entre agriculteurs comme levier [SOURCE: Page_98]

d’action environnemental. Les résultats de la Tableau 6 révèlent que seulement 12, 8 % des AAC organisent des échanges permanents de fonciers entre les agriculteurs pour [SOURCE: Page_98]

préserver les AAC. En revanche aucune AAC de l’enquête n’a organisé d’échanges temporaires de parcelles. Plusieurs animateurs affirment toutefois avoir connaissance [SOURCE: Page_98]

de cette pratique au niveau des agriculteurs. [SOURCE: Page_98]

On retrouve la pratique des échanges temporaires de parcelles entre agriculteurs dans plusieurs régions en France. D’après les réponses des animateurs de l’enquête en ligne, dans les départements du Gard et de l’Hérault, les échanges de parcelles se font principalement entre les céréaliers et les meloniers. Dans le Pas-de-Calais, les échanges [SOURCE: Page_98]

temporaires de parcelles se font pour produire la pomme de terre. En Normandie, les [SOURCE: Page_98]

agriculteurs y ont recours pour la culture du lin et de la pomme de terre. En Isère, les échanges se font dans les bassins de production de la rose de plein champ. D’après la [SOURCE: Page_98]

réglementation qui encadre la pratique des échanges de parcelles (code rural français dans ses articles L. 17-1 et L. 411-39), l'échange se fait par simple accord verbal entre deux agriculteurs. La durée de l’entente peut se limiter à une année ou au contraire [SOURCE: Page_98]

100 [SOURCE: Page_98]

perdurer dans le temps en fonction des objectifs des agriculteurs. Concernant les [SOURCE: Page_99]

négociations entre agriculteurs, elles sont aux dires des animateurs informelles et la confiance entre les deux parties suffit à conclure l'échange. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Deux raisons principales ont été évoquées par les animateurs pour justifier la pratique des échanges temporaires de parcelles entre les agriculteurs. Il s’agit de la nécessité de [SOURCE: Page_99]

diversifier les cultures dans les rotations pour permettre de rallonger le délai de retour de certaines cultures et la volonté d’agrandir les surfaces de certaines cultures de [SOURCE: Page_99]

l’assolement. En effet, les agriculteurs auraient recours aux échanges de parcelles pour diversifier leurs rotations, en vue de lever la contrainte des délais de retours sur [SOURCE: Page_99]

certaines cultures. Par exemple, les échanges temporaires de parcelles entre les céréaliers et producteurs de pomme de terre seraient le moyen pour les « patatiers » [SOURCE: Page_99]

de rallonger le délai de retour de la pomme de terre sur les parcelles et pour les céréaliers de limiter la récurrence du blé sur certaines parcelles. Elles permettraient [SOURCE: Page_99]

aussi à certains agriculteurs d’augmenter la surface de certaines cultures de l’assolement grâce à l’externalisation d’une partie de l’assolement. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Les raisons évoquées par les animateurs territoriaux en charge des AAC traduisent la [SOURCE: Page_99]

valeur agronomique de la pratique des échanges temporaires de parcelles et soulignent le besoin de mieux comprendre les conditions de recours par les agriculteurs. Par exemple, les échanges entre les producteurs de pommes de terre ou encore de melon et les céréaliers permettraient aux premiers de gérer la contrainte [SOURCE: Page_99]

agronomique du délai de retour de leur culture et pour les seconds d’introduire une nouvelle culture (généralement une tête de rotation) dans la succession et de profiter [SOURCE: Page_99]

des reliquats laissés par la culture précédente. Ainsi, sur les parcelles concernées, on [SOURCE: Page_99]

peut avoir des successions de culture qui peuvent être intéressantes de par la diversification des cultures plus fortes que l’on n’aurait pas sans échanges de parcelles. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Lorsque les éleveurs et les céréaliers s’échangent temporairement les parcelles, les bénéfices sont réciproques dans la mesure où les éleveurs profitent des parcelles mises en jachère par les céréaliers pour récolter du fourrage tandis que les céréaliers de leur [SOURCE: Page_99]

101 [SOURCE: Page_99]

côté profitent de parcelles assainies (sur le plan des adventices) en retournant les [SOURCE: Page_100]

prairies temporaires des éleveurs. Ces raisons font des échanges temporaires de foncier un possible levier d’action environnemental dans la mesure où lors des échanges, des [SOURCE: Page_100]

lots de parcelles peuvent changer de systèmes de culture et on peut avoir une plus grande diversification des cultures sur les parcelles échangées et donc une [SOURCE: Page_100]

modification locale de la pression agricole sur le milieu (Marie et al. 2009; Steinmann and Dobers 2013). Ces informations sont toutefois fois à relativiser dans la mesure où [SOURCE: Page_100]

il s’agit de l’avis des animateurs qui ne sont pas des agriculteurs. Ces informations [SOURCE: Page_100]

doivent être confirmées auprès des principaux concernés avant toute généralisation. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Au-delà des raisons agronomiques sus évoquées, l’autre intérêt de faire des échanges temporaires de parcelles un levier d’action au niveau des AAC est propre à la démarche [SOURCE: Page_100]

qui émane des agriculteurs eux-mêmes. Il s’agit notamment des possibilités que ces échanges offrent en termes d’organisation (moins d’acteurs impliqués) avec une [SOURCE: Page_100]

période de négociation relativement courte, et de la flexibilité de l’action (possibilité pour chaque agriculteur de récupérer ses parcelles à l’issue de l’échange). Ces échanges [SOURCE: Page_100]

permettraient aux animateurs d’AAC de passer par des procédures connues des agriculteurs ce qui faciliterait la légitimation de l’action (Bonnefond and Fournier 2013). [SOURCE: Page_100]

La tradition des échanges temporaires de foncier au niveau de certains territoires et la coordination de fait qui existe entre les agriculteurs qui s’échangent temporairement le foncier pourrait fournir une garantie quant à l’adhésion des agriculteurs si cet axe [SOURCE: Page_100]

leur est proposé. Les animateurs d’AAC pourraient par exemple s’appuyer sur les outils existants de facilitation des échanges de parcelles à l’exemple de l’outil « la bonne [SOURCE: Page_100]

parcelle » développé par la SAFER pour organiser les échanges temporaires de [SOURCE: Page_100]

parcelles à caractère environnemental entre les agriculteurs. Reste toutefois la question de savoir comment orienter ces échanges pour aller dans le sens d'une amélioration [SOURCE: Page_100]

de la qualité des eaux. [SOURCE: Page_100]

L’échange temporaire de parcelles entre agriculteurs tel qu’il est présenté ici , pourrait ainsi devenir une alternative à l’acquisition des parcelles en pleine propriété, laquelle [SOURCE: Page_100]

102 [SOURCE: Page_100]

entraîne de facto l’achat de l’intégralité des droits d’usage qui lui sont rattachés (Pivot [SOURCE: Page_101]

et Aznar 2000) et génère des tensions avec les agriculteurs (Barataud et al. 2014). Une connaissance approfondie des mécanismes de ces échanges et des motivations des [SOURCE: Page_101]

agriculteurs à recourir aux échanges temporaires de parcelles est toutefois nécessaire avant une éventuelle mobilisation lors de la gestion foncière des AAC. [SOURCE: Page_101]

4. Conclusions La question de l’efficacité du foncier comme levier d’action environnemental reste centrale dans la littérature scientifique récente traitant des problématiques de [SOURCE: Page_101]

préservation de la ressource en eau en France. Le diagnostic des gestions foncières actuelles des AAC présenté dans ce chapitre permet de mettre en lumière les [SOURCE: Page_101]

dynamiques foncières en cours au niveau des AAC. Il apparaît que les animateurs territoriaux en charge de la protection de l’eau potable mobilisent une diversité d’outils [SOURCE: Page_101]

fonciers dans le cadre de leur mission. Les principaux outils fonciers actuellement mobilisés se composent de : l’acquisition de parcelles à l’intérieur ou à l’extérieur de [SOURCE: Page_101]

l’AAC, la mise en place de mesures agro-environnementales (MAE) ou encore la contractualisation des obligations réelles environnementales. Les parcelles acquises [SOURCE: Page_101]

servent principalement à instaurer des baux ruraux environnementaux ou encore à l’installation des jeunes en agriculture biologique. Ce qui semble cohérent avec la [SOURCE: Page_101]

volonté des autorités publiques françaises d’encourager l’agriculture biologique et l’agriculture faiblement utilisatrice d’intrants dans les zones présentant des problèmes [SOURCE: Page_101]

de qualité de l'eau afin de prévenir la pollution agricole diffuse (Vincent and Fleury 2015; Garnier et al. 2016). Les animateurs peuvent aussi organiser des échanges [SOURCE: Page_101]

permanents de fonciers avec les agriculteurs. Par contre, aucun animateur de l’échantillon d’enquête n’organise d’échanges temporaires de parcelles entre [SOURCE: Page_101]

agriculteurs [SOURCE: Page_101]

Il apparaît aussi que la gestion foncière des AAC reste complexe à mettre en œuvre. En [SOURCE: Page_101]

effet, pour utile qu’il soit, le levier foncier fait face à plusieurs difficultés qui sont susceptibles de compromettre son efficacité. Ces difficultés sont essentiellement liées [SOURCE: Page_101]

103 [SOURCE: Page_101]

à la question de la disponibilité foncière et de son coût. Comme le soulignent Barataud [SOURCE: Page_102]

et Hellec (2015), l’intervention sur le marché du foncier agricole engendre des conflits durables entre agriculteurs du territoire. Par ailleurs, l’acquisition de foncier par l’acteur [SOURCE: Page_102]

public contribue à l’augmentation du prix du foncier (Bonnefond and Fournier 2013). Ces difficultés suggèrent aussi d’explorer de nouvelles façons d’améliorer l’efficacité [SOURCE: Page_102]

des outils fonciers à proposer aux animateurs en charge des AAC. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Partant de ce constat et dans une perspective d’amélioration du levier foncier, il peut [SOURCE: Page_102]

être intéressant d’envisager l’exploration d’autres leviers fonciers à l’instar de la pratique des échanges temporaires de parcelles entre agriculteurs comme solution [SOURCE: Page_102]

d’atténuation des tensions que peut engendrer les autres outils fonciers. La tradition des échanges au niveau de certains territoires et la complicité qui existe entre les [SOURCE: Page_102]

agriculteurs, qui s’échangent temporairement les parcelles, donne potentiellement une garantie quant à l’adhésion des agriculteurs si ce levier leur est proposé. Un tel outil [SOURCE: Page_102]

s’appuyant sur les pratiques et logiques des agriculteurs est à même de fournir de nouvelles marges de manœuvres aux animateurs territoriaux. Des recherches [SOURCE: Page_102]

complémentaires sont toutefois nécessaires pour préciser les conditions dans lesquelles les échanges temporaires de parcelles entre agriculteurs peuvent avoir un [SOURCE: Page_102]

intérêt environnemental au niveau des AAC. [SOURCE: Page_102]

104 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Synthèse Partie 1 : Le diagnostic des pratiques foncières agricoles à but environnemental aux échelles internationales et Françaises entrepris dans cette première partie permet de : [SOURCE: Page_103]

I) Mettre en évidence au moyen de l’analyse bibliographique, l’existence d’une diversité de pratiques foncières agricoles qui peuvent être regroupées en différentes catégories selon qu’elles sont basées sur des stratégies ascendantes issues d'initiatives d'agriculteurs locaux (exemple : location de parcelles pour améliorer la structure de l'exploitation.) ou sur des stratégies descendantes émanant d'organismes publics ou d'organisations privées (exemple : politiques publiques ayant un impact sur les droits d’usage des terres par le biais de la réglementation). On retient aussi la faible prise en compte dans la littérature des aspects environnementaux des pratiques foncières en général et des échanges temporaires de parcelles entre agriculteurs en particulier. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Ii) Cerner la place actuelle des outils fonciers mobilisés pour la protection de la ressource ; grâce à la double enquête par questionnaire et par entretien semi-dirigé adressées aux animateurs territoriaux en charge de la gestion des AAC. En effet, dans les AAC, il existe plusieurs dispositifs fonciers dont les échanges permanents de parcelles (les animateurs de sites peuvent organiser de tels échanges) entre agriculteurs. Les animateurs ont aussi la possibilité d’acquérir le foncier en propriété. Aucune disposition ne permet cependant d’organiser les échanges temporaires de parcelles, même si leur existence est connue de certains animateurs. [SOURCE: Page_103]

105 [SOURCE: Page_103]

## PARTIE$_2$ : COMPRENDRE LES MOTIVATIONS AGRICOLES DES ÉCHANGES TEMPORAIRES DE PARCELLES

La partie précédente s’est attachée à positionner la problématique de la thèse aux [SOURCE: Page_104]

échelles internationales et françaises. On retient de cette phase exploratoire, qu’en France, plusieurs outils fonciers sont à la disposition des gestionnaires d’AAC qui [SOURCE: Page_104]

peuvent les mobiliser dans le cadre de leur mission. Les principaux outils fonciers actuellement mobilisés se composent de l’acquisition de parcelles à l’intérieur ou à [SOURCE: Page_104]

l’extérieur de l’AAC, la mise en place de mesures agro-environnementales (MAE) ou encore la contractualisation des obligations réelles environnementales. Les échanges [SOURCE: Page_104]

temporaires de parcelle entre agriculteurs ne font pas encore partie des outils fonciers mobilisés par les animateurs que nous avons enquêtés. Ceux-ci évoquent toutefois la [SOURCE: Page_104]

tradition des échanges temporaires dans certains territoires, pour des raisons de diversification des rotations notamment. [SOURCE: Page_104]

L’objectif de cette partie est d’approfondir la compréhension des motivations agricoles des échanges temporaires de parcelles entre agriculteurs amorcée dans la précédente [SOURCE: Page_104]

partie. Elle mobilise les connaissances déjà formalisées pour répondre aux questionnements suivants : Comment fonctionnent concrètement les échanges de [SOURCE: Page_104]

parcelles entre agriculteurs ? Quelles peuvent être les motivations agronomiques et socio-économiques des échanges ? Et qui sont les agriculteurs concernés ? [SOURCE: Page_104]

La partie comporte deux chapitres : le premier chapitre (correspond au chapitre 3 du [SOURCE: Page_104]

manuscrit) présente la démarche d’identification du site devant servir de cadre d’analyse pour étudier les échanges temporaires de parcelles. Le deuxième chapitre [SOURCE: Page_104]

(correspond au chapitre 4 du manuscrit) détaille les mécanismes des échanges et les motivations agronomiques, économiques et sociales derrière la pratique des échanges [SOURCE: Page_104]

temporaires de foncier entre agriculteurs sur le site d’étude choisi. [SOURCE: Page_104]

106 [SOURCE: Page_104]

Chapitre 3 : Choix du site d’étude Dans ce chapitre je présente la démarche déployée pour choisir le site d’étude pour [SOURCE: Page_105]

l’analyse des échanges temporaires de parcelles dans une perspective agricole et [SOURCE: Page_105]

environnementale. Le chapitre est organisé en trois parties. La première partie expose la méthode d’identification des exploitations potentiellement concernées par les [SOURCE: Page_105]

échanges. La deuxième partie explique la manière dont le site de l’AAC d’Escrebieux comme site d’étude a été repéré. Enfin, la troisième partie présente les caractéristiques [SOURCE: Page_105]

de l’AAC et justifie sa pertinence pour notre étude. [SOURCE: Page_105]

107 [SOURCE: Page_105]

1. Identification des exploitations potentiellement concernées par les échanges temporaires de parcelles: mobilisation des données du RPG à l’échelle de la France entière. [SOURCE: Page_106]

### 1.1.       Les données du registre parcellaire graphique (RPG) comme données d’entrée

Le Registre Parcellaire Graphique (RPG) est une base de données géo-référencée à [SOURCE: Page_106]

haute résolution (1:5000) qui renseigne sur la géométrie des parcelles et l’occupation du sol. Il est basé sur la déclaration des agriculteurs pour les subventions de la politique [SOURCE: Page_106]

agricole commune. L'occupation du sol est décrite à l'aide d'une nomenclature qui, depuis 2015, comprend environ 350 cultures. Les données du RPG sont disponibles en [SOURCE: Page_106]

France au niveau des directions régionales de l’agriculture, de l’alimentation et de la forêt (DRAAF) Elles sont accessibles à tous les usagers. Trois niveaux de communication [SOURCE: Page_106]

des données du RPG sont disponibles à savoir le niveau 1, le niveau 2 et le niveau 3. Selon le niveau, les type d’information n’est pas le même. Le niveau 2, par exemple, [SOURCE: Page_106]

permet de connaitre l’identifiant PACAGE12 de l’exploitation, ce qui n’est pas le cas avec [SOURCE: Page_106]

les données de niveau^1. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Nous travaillons ici avec les données du niveau 2. Pour chaque parcelle, le nom de la culture et l'identifiant PACAGE de l'agriculteur sont indiqués. Les parcelles ayant le [SOURCE: Page_106]

même numéro pour une année donnée sont déclarées par la même exploitation fournissant ainsi des informations sur les caractéristiques des exploitations agricoles (Grandgirard and Zielinski 2008; Piet and Cariou 2014). Le RPG est édité annuellement et couvre l'ensemble du territoire national. Ainsi, il est possible d’avoir les assolements [SOURCE: Page_106]

des agriculteurs sur plusieurs années. Il est une source importante d’informations grâce à la possibilité qu’il donne d’avoir l’historique des assolements des agriculteurs. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Identifiant attribué à un agriculteur qui bénéficie des aides de la Politique Agricole 12 [SOURCE: Page_106]

Commune (PAC) [SOURCE: Page_106]

108 [SOURCE: Page_106]

Dans le cadre de la thèse, les données du RPG sont mobilisées comme données [SOURCE: Page_107]

d’entrée pour identifier les exploitations potentiellement concernées par les échanges temporaires de parcelle en France. [SOURCE: Page_107]

### 1.2.       Identification des exploitations concernées par les échanges temporaires de parcelles.

La démarche d’identification des exploitations concernées par des échanges de parcelles à l’année, à partir des données du RPG à l’échelle de la France entière a été [SOURCE: Page_107]

développée par Philippe Martin (UMR SADAPT). Cette méthode repose sur l’hypothèse que les échanges de parcelles entre agriculteurs sont portés par des cultures à forte [SOURCE: Page_107]

valeur ajoutée pour lesquelles on sait qu’il y a des contraintes agronomiques sur les délais de retour sur une même parcelle. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Pour ces cultures, on soupçonne qu’il y a des échanges de parcelles lorsque [SOURCE: Page_107]

l’agriculteur atteint une certaine proportion de sole. (Par exemple : pour une exploitation productrice de pomme de terre et dont la surface agricole utile est égale [SOURCE: Page_107]

à 100 ha, la sole maximale de pomme de terre possible chaque année est de 25 ha, en considérant que cette exploitation respecte un délai de retour de quatre ans pour la [SOURCE: Page_107]

pomme de terre. Si cette exploitation produit plus de 25 ha de pomme de terre, elle fera face à la contrainte agronomique sur le délai de retour). [SOURCE: Page_107]

La démarche consiste à calculer les assolements pour chacune des cultures déclarées pour l’ensemble des exploitations de France. Un script script R a été établi pour le calcul [SOURCE: Page_107]

des assolements. Les données RPG de 2019 ont servi au calcul des assolements. On a en sortie un fichier avec l’assolement de chaque exploitation agrégé au niveau national. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Lorsque la sole d’une culture comme la pomme de terre est supérieure à 60% de la SAU, cela implique que, compte tenu des contraintes de délai de retour de cette [SOURCE: Page_107]

culture, l’agriculteur aura du mal à faire tourner la culture dans son exploitation sans recourir aux échanges de parcelles. A l’aide du logiciel de SIG (QGIS), on peut obtenir [SOURCE: Page_107]

109 [SOURCE: Page_107]

les couches vectorielles (Shape files) des exploitations concernées (la démarche [SOURCE: Page_108]

d’extraction des couches est présentée en annexe 8). [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 1.3.       Application de la méthode sur des cultures considérées à forte valeur

ajoutée [SOURCE: Page_108]

Le travail de calcul des assolements à l’échelle de la France s’est fait dans un premier [SOURCE: Page_108]

temps sur trois cultures, à savoir le lin, la pomme de terre et le melon. Il a par la suite été élargi à d’autres cultures, notamment le tabac, le chanvre et le radis, grâce au travail [SOURCE: Page_108]

des étudiants de 3eme année d’AgroParisTech que j’ai co-encadrés dans le cadre de leur projet d’ingénieur. Seules les exploitations avec les soles de cultures supérieures à [SOURCE: Page_108]

60% de l’assolement nous intéressent. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Dans le cadre de ma thèse, j’ai choisi de m’intéresser aux exploitations productrices de pommes de terre qui est une culture à forte valeur ajoutée majoritairement produite [SOURCE: Page_108]

dans le nord de la France et qui demande un délai de retour d’au moins 4 ans. Elle a été choisie au regard de la tradition des échanges sur cette culture déjà évoquée dans [SOURCE: Page_108]

la littérature. Pour localiser les exploitations ayant plus de 60% de sole de pommes de terre dans leur assolement, j’ai utilisé le logiciel QGIS. J’ai ainsi pu obtenir la distribution [SOURCE: Page_108]

à l’échelle de la France des exploitations pour lesquelles la sole de pomme de terre est supérieure ou égale à 60%. La Figure           ci-dessous présente la répartition de ces [SOURCE: Page_108]

exploitations à l’échelle de la France Métropolitaine. Sur cette figure, on constate une forte concentration des exploitations à 60% d’assolement en pomme de terre dans plusieurs départements de France notamment le Pas-de-Calais et le Nord. [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 2. Identification du site de l’AAC d’Escrebieux

On propose de superposer, d’une part, la carte de distribution des exploitations [SOURCE: Page_110]

spécialisées dans la production de pomme de terre (60% de l’assolement) à l’échelle de la France entière à, d’autre part, la carte des AAC recensées en France métropolitaine [SOURCE: Page_110]

(carte disponible sur le site internet : https://www.sandre.eaufrance.fr/Rechercher-un- jeu-de-donnees.). (Voir Figure 10). D’après la Figure 10, plusieurs AAC sont concernées [SOURCE: Page_110]

par des exploitations spécialisées dans la production 60% de pommes de terre. Étant donnée la proximité géographique des départements du Pas-de-Calais et du Nord [SOURCE: Page_110]

avec la région Ile-de-France, (ce qui sur le plan logistique facilitait les déplacements, nous avons fait le choix de nous intéresser aux AAC situées dans ces deux [SOURCE: Page_110]

départements. Le site de l’AAC d’Escrebieux est caractérisé par une plus forte concentration d’exploitations spécialisées dans la pomme de terre. [SOURCE: Page_110]

### 3. Justification du choix de l’AAC d’Escrebieux comme site d’Etude

L’AAC d’ESCREBIEUX est située à cheval entre le département du Pas-de-Calais et le [SOURCE: Page_112]

département du Nord. Elle s’étend sur environ 10 000 ha dont 80 % sont considérés comme agricoles. Elle couvre les communes de Cuincy, Esquerchin, Flers-en- [SOURCE: Page_112]

Escrebieux, Lauwin-Planque et Douai (pour le département du Nord) ; Biache-Saint- Vaast, Fresnes-lès-Montauban, Izel-lès-Équerchin, Neuvireuil, Quiéry-la-Motte, Vitry- [SOURCE: Page_112]

### 3.2.     Historique des échanges de parcelles            sur le territoire de l’AAC d’Escrebieux

D’après les acteurs du territoire de l’AAC d’Escrebieux que nous avons enquêtés, la [SOURCE: Page_116]

pratique des échanges de parcelles entre agriculteurs est ancienne sur le territoire de [SOURCE: Page_116]

l’AAC d’Escrebieux. Elle a débuté avec les producteurs d’endives. Elle a progressivement glissé vers la production de pommes de terre lorsque de nombreux endiviers se sont [SOURCE: Page_116]

convertis à cette culture, encouragés par l’arrivée de gros industriels dans la région, à l’instar de l’entreprise McCain ou encore de Carrefour. L’arrivée des industriels, couplée [SOURCE: Page_116]

au dernier remembrement ayant eu lieu au nord du l’AAC (communes de Douai, Cuincy, etc.) au début des années 1990, aurait favorisé la hausse de la demande en pommes [SOURCE: Page_116]

de terre et les agriculteurs des communes concernées se seraient spécialisés dans cette production avec des investissements importants dans le matériel et les hangars. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Cette spécialisation aurait entraîné les agriculteurs concernés dans une logique [SOURCE: Page_116]

d’agrandissement de leur exploitation pour permettre d’augmenter les surfaces en pommes de terre. C’est ainsi, que la recherche de nouvelles parcelles à progressivement [SOURCE: Page_116]

dépassé les limites de leur commune, pour s’étendre plus au sud de l’AAC. Le sud de l’AAC, majoritairement occupée par des polyculteurs éleveurs, qui jusqu’ici [SOURCE: Page_116]

connaissaient une dynamique d’échanges parcellaires moindre, est alors investi par les agriculteurs producteurs de pomme de terre venus du Nord. C’est ainsi que [SOURCE: Page_116]

l’intensification des échanges de parcelles s’est propagée au sud de l’AAC et continue de progresser. Elle dépasse aujourd’hui les limites de l’AAC et du département du Pas- [SOURCE: Page_116]

de-Calais. [SOURCE: Page_116]

119 [SOURCE: Page_117]

CHAPITRE 4: TEMPORARY PLOT EXCHANGES AS COORDINATED STRATEGY BETWEEN FARMERS FOR ALLOCATION OF CROPS: LESSONS LEARNED FROM A CATCHMENT AREA IN FRANCE [SOURCE: Page_118]

Dans ce nouveau chapitre, je présente le travail entrepris sur l’AAC d’Escrebieux qui a été choisie comme site d’étude pour comprendre comment fonctionnent [SOURCE: Page_118]

concrètement les échanges temporaires de parcelles entre agriculteurs et connaître leurs motivations agronomiques, sociales et économiques. Le dispositif mis en place [SOURCE: Page_118]

pour ce travail combine l’analyse des données du RPG pour les années 2015 à 2019 et les enquêtes en exploitation. Les résultats de ce travail et les enseignements qui en [SOURCE: Page_118]

découlent sont détaillés dans le chapitre. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Le chapitre est rédigé en anglais et présenté sous forme d’article. Il sera soumis au journal « Land Use Policy » [SOURCE: Page_118]

120 [SOURCE: Page_118]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_119]

Temporary plot exchanges are the transfers of plots between farmers that result in temporary changes in the land use rights. At the landscape level, temporary plot [SOURCE: Page_119]

exchanges lead to new spatial and temporal organisations of cropping systems co- [SOURCE: Page_119]

designed between the farmers who have used the plots in one year or another. These exchanges of plots are very little studied although understanding their main socio- [SOURCE: Page_119]

technical reasons and their agronomic consequences can help to better assess the environmental pressure of agricultural activity, especially in the water catchment areas [SOURCE: Page_119]

where water quality issues are important. Using an analytical framework of landscape agronomy, this work aimed to explore the socio-technical reasons of temporary plot [SOURCE: Page_119]

exchanges. We used (1) the French Land Parcel Identification System (LPIS) database, which provides quasi-continuous, broad coverage of agricultural areas in France, and [SOURCE: Page_119]

(2) semi-structured surveys to question the socio-technical reasons of temporary plot exchanges at the farm and landscape levels. Through this methodological framework, [SOURCE: Page_119]

we present the dynamics and diversity of temporary plot exchanges within one French water catchment area. We have distinguished plot exchanges according to the [SOURCE: Page_119]

reciprocity of the exchange. Exchanged plots can be bilateral, one-sided, or bilateral but deferred. We highlighted that temporary plot exchanges are part of a reasoning [SOURCE: Page_119]

strategy for crop allocation that is coordinated between several farmers. This strategy allows for greater crop diversification and leads to a cultivated mosaic, which emerges [SOURCE: Page_119]

from the spatial organisation of the cropping system on the plots. These results, which may be relevant for other countries where there is a tradition of plot exchanges, [SOURCE: Page_119]

indicate for the first time that knowledge of temporary plot exchanges and their socio- technical reasons can help build a better understanding of agricultural activities on a [SOURCE: Page_119]

territorial scale and thus promote better land coordination when implementing environmental actions. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Keywords: Allocation of crops, socio-technical reasons, cropping system, LPIS, landscape agronomy [SOURCE: Page_119]

### 1. Introduction

Intensive agriculture is recognised as being responsible for several aspects of [SOURCE: Page_120]

ecosystem degradation, including degradation of water quality, reduction of biodiversity and depletion of non-renewable resources (Millennium Ecosystem [SOURCE: Page_120]

Assessment 2005). The current dynamics of agricultural intensification will continue to heighten this degradation if adequate measures are not taken (Evans et al. 2019; OECD [SOURCE: Page_120]

2020). [SOURCE: Page_120]

The Agronomic research on limiting agricultural pollution covers a wide range of [SOURCE: Page_120]

subjects with varying spatial scales. The plot scale makes it possible to examine cropping systems, i.e. all the technical methods (cultivation techniques and crop succession) (Sebillotte 1990; Leteinturier et al. 2007). The farm level allows to recommend actions to be taken at the level where cropping systems are decided (Papy [SOURCE: Page_120]

2001; Joannon et al. 2005). Beyond the farm, some research examines strategies at the landscape level (Benoît et al. 1997; Chopin et al. 2017), enabling consideration of the [SOURCE: Page_120]

spatial organisation of different farm territories (Marie et al. 2009) and their temporal dynamics (Bonin and Lardon 2002; Vasseur et al. 2013;Bouty 2015; Barbottin et al. 2018) [SOURCE: Page_120]

to formalise the interdependencies between cropping systems, the resulting landscape configurations and the farmers' room for changing production methods ; Papy and [SOURCE: Page_120]

Torre 2002). [SOURCE: Page_120]

The environmental problems in agriculture affect areas that are often large, intersecting the farm landscape of a multitude of farms, the management of which contributes to [SOURCE: Page_120]

that of the landscape and its natural resources (Benoît et al. 1999; Martin et al. 2006; Dury et al. 2013). Farm territory dynamics are important to consider when assessing [SOURCE: Page_120]

the environmental impact of agricultural activities (Zangue et al. 2022). These impacts have been addressed in previous studies dealing with farmland management at the [SOURCE: Page_120]

territorial level, but these studies focus on the general reconfiguration of agricultural plots over multi-year periods (Bouty 2015; Preux 2019), for example as a result of an expansion of the farm. There are other, more discreet evolutions in which farmers [SOURCE: Page_120]

122 [SOURCE: Page_120]

exchange plots for a limited time (1 to 3 years), which we designate as temporary plot [SOURCE: Page_121]

exchanges. Such plots are managed with cropping systems that are co-designed by different farmers who have used the plots in one year or another. [SOURCE: Page_121]

The practice of temporary plot exchanges is widespread among many farmers around the world. Indeed, plot exchanges between farmers have been reported in several [SOURCE: Page_121]

countries, including potato growers in Argentina Pampa (Salembier et al. 2016), in Northern France (Wünsch 2004) and in Belguim (Rouget 2008) or melon growers in [SOURCE: Page_121]

southern France (Clément et al. 2019). Despite the tradition of temporary parcel exchanges recognised in several agricultural areas, their socio-technical reasons and [SOURCE: Page_121]

the resulting agronomic consequences remain little known by the academic research. However, the fact that a plot passes from one farm to another may lead to a change in [SOURCE: Page_121]

the cropping system. This can be expected to lead to a cultivated mosaic at landscape level and therefore potentially less impactful crop successions than those encountered [SOURCE: Page_121]

on each of the farms individually. [SOURCE: Page_121]

This study explored the sociotechnical reasons and conditions of temporary plot exchanges for an environmental purpose. We addressed the following question: What [SOURCE: Page_121]

are the main socio-technical reasons of temporary plot exchanges between farmers? We hypothesized that temporary plot exchanges between farmers are part of cropping plan strategy when farmers find themselves under pressure because they are saturated with crops, some of which may require relatively long return periods. [SOURCE: Page_121]

The next part of this article sheds light on this research question. First, we present the analytical framework (section 2), the methodology (section 3) and the results of our [SOURCE: Page_121]

work on characterising plot exchanges and understanding the underlying agricultural logic (section 4). We then discuss the methodological contributions of our work and [SOURCE: Page_121]

the prospects offered by temporary plot exchanges as a potential lever for environmental action (section 5). [SOURCE: Page_121]

### 2. Analytical Framework

Temporary plot exchanges between farmers refers to bilateral or multilateral transfers [SOURCE: Page_122]

of plots between farmers that result in temporary changes in the land use rights. It is considered as an example of farmland management practices defined by Zangue et [SOURCE: Page_122]

al.(2022), as actions that leads to changes in both land use and land-use rights. According to several authors the use of exchange of plots or renting occur when [SOURCE: Page_122]

farmers meet agronomic constraints like phytosanitary issues or breaking the cycle of weeds (Choumert and Phélinas 2015). [SOURCE: Page_122]

The plot exchanges considered in this study differ from joint crop rotation (Gabriel et al. 2019) in that there is no polling of land use rights but rather a temporary transfer of the use right of the part of the farm landscape concerned by the exchanges. In France, the procedure for the exchange of plot temporarily is relatively simple: a verbal [SOURCE: Page_122]

agreement between the two farmers wishing to temporarily exchanges plots is sufficient. There is no transfer of lease obligations (e.g. lease payments) for tenant [SOURCE: Page_122]

farmers, as the lessee is responsible for the breaches of their co-exchanger. The two farmers agree on the duration of the exchange. [SOURCE: Page_122]

To understand the socio-technical reasons of temporary plot exchanges between farms, we developed an analytical framework by combining two classic agronomic methods that are generally used separately: on-farm surveys and quantitative data [SOURCE: Page_122]

processing. This hybrid system (Schaller 2011; Debolini 2021) is part of the corpus of approaches developed in landscape agronomy to address the environmental problems [SOURCE: Page_122]

in agricultural activities (Schaller 2011; Bouty 2015; Barbottin et al. 2018). [SOURCE: Page_122]

Landscape agronomy is the study of the relationships between farming systems and [SOURCE: Page_122]

agricultural landscape dynamics (Moonen et al. 2010; Benoît et al. 2012; Rizzo et al. 2012). It deals with the spatial organisation of cropping systems and their possible [SOURCE: Page_122]

environmental impacts (Martin et al. 2006). The landscape agronomy approach makes it possible to combine several sources of data e.g. spatialized data that can be compared with data from field surveys to study the links between agricultural activities [SOURCE: Page_122]

124 [SOURCE: Page_122]

and the environment at different spatial scales (Benoît et al. 2012; Sylvie Lardon et al. [SOURCE: Page_123]

2012). By applying this approach to the issue of temporary plot exchange practices, we wished to identify how farmers design their technical systems on a given site by [SOURCE: Page_123]

including temporary plot exchanges. [SOURCE: Page_123]

### 3.1.       Study area

The Escrebieux catchment area is located in Northern France and covers a total area of 9,450 ha. It is an area straddling the departments of Pas de Calais and Nord (see Figure [SOURCE: Page_123]

13). The surface area covered by the French LPIS is 6,329 ha, which represents 67% of the total area of the catchment area (the remaining areas are mainly urbanised). About [SOURCE: Page_123]

146 farms had at least one parcel in the Escrebieux catchment area and among these farmers, 65 of them had all their land in the Escrebieux catchment area. [SOURCE: Page_123]

The agricultural landscape is dominated by soft winter wheat, which occupies 45% of [SOURCE: Page_123]

the agricultural area, followed by sugar beet with 15%, potatoes with 12%. Potato farming benefits from strong demand in the agri-food industries present in the region. [SOURCE: Page_123]

The Escrebieux catchment area was chosen as the study area because of the presence [SOURCE: Page_123]

of several farms where potatoes account for more than 60% of the cropping plan; indicating tension in terms of the agronomic constraints of the return time (Martin and [SOURCE: Page_123]

Zangue, unpublished work). It was also chosen because it is representative of the issues involved in preserving water resources. It is classified as a priority area in that actions to restore water quality must be carried out according to the Water Act. The high level [SOURCE: Page_123]

of agricultural activity in the area leads to pollution by nitrates and pesticides, the concentrations of which are higher than the legal maximum threshold (50 mg/l of water [SOURCE: Page_123]

for nitrates and 0.50 µg/L of water for total pesticides). The southern zone of the catchment area is the most fragile because of the chalk table, which outcrops with no clay layer to protect the soil from infiltration and leaching. The northern zone is [SOURCE: Page_123]

125 [SOURCE: Page_123]

somewhat better protected as it is covered by a thin clay layer (Ansart and Gaudré [SOURCE: Page_124]

2009). [SOURCE: Page_124]

### 3.2.       French Land Parcel Identification System

The LPIS, known as Registre Parcellaire Graphique (RPG) in French, is a high-resolution (1:5000) georeferenced database that describes field geometry and land cover [SOURCE: Page_124]

(Grandgirard and Zielinski 2008; Piet and Cariou 2014). It is based on farmers’ declaration for the Common Agriculture Policy subsidies. Land cover is described using [SOURCE: Page_124]

a nomenclature of approximately 350 crops. The RPG data are available in France at regional level. Three levels of communication [SOURCE: Page_124]

of the RPG data are available. Depending on the level, the type of information is not the same. Level 2, for example, allows the farm's identifier to be obtained, which is not [SOURCE: Page_124]

the case with level 1 data. We are working here with level 2 data. For each plot, the crop name and farmer identification are given. Plots with the same number in a given [SOURCE: Page_124]

year belong to the same holding. Since 2015, the database has consisted of a shapefile containing parcel block geometry and each parcel block’s unique identifiers. The LPIS [SOURCE: Page_124]

is edited yearly and covers the whole national territory. It is therefore possible to follow the plot dynamics of farms in yearly intervals based on their identifiers (Barbottin et al. [SOURCE: Page_124]

2018). [SOURCE: Page_124]

### 3.3.1. Analysis of LPIS data with RPG Explorer software

We used the LPIS data to observe plot exchanges between farms with the help of RPG [SOURCE: Page_124]

Explorer software (Levavasseur et al. (2016). From the LPIS data, the software analyses the agricultural landscape dynamics by deriving the crop proportions for each farm, [SOURCE: Page_124]

crop sequences at the plot level and models crop rotations of a given study area (e.g. catchment, administrative unit). At the farm level, it is also possible to determine farm [SOURCE: Page_124]

area expansion or reduction from the geographical overlay of farm plots for a given period (Barbottin et al. 2018) [SOURCE: Page_124]

126 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Figure 13: Localization of the Escrebieux catchment area situation in northern France, straddling the departments of Pas de Calais and Nord. [SOURCE: Page_125]

127 [SOURCE: Page_125]

i.   Observation of plot exchanges between farms with LPIS data [SOURCE: Page_126]

By running RPG Explorer with LPIS data from the Escrebieux catchment area (data from [SOURCE: Page_126]

2015 to 2019) we were able to observe plot transfers between farms and determine the surface concerned by these transfers (annual loss or gain of plots). Then, we identified [SOURCE: Page_126]

the farms with ‘boomerang plots’, or plots that move from one farm to another before returning to the original farm in the following year or the year after. In the historical [SOURCE: Page_126]

record of the farm, this plot will be present in year n, absent for one or more years, and present again in year n + 2 or n + 3 (see Figure 14). The groups of farms that are linked [SOURCE: Page_126]

to each other through plot transfers were called ‘clusters’ (Martin et al. 2018). [SOURCE: Page_126]

The data from RPG Explorer were stored and processed with MS Excel and R: statistics of the total crop area exchanged were calculated from the data using Excel, and the [SOURCE: Page_127]

total boomerang plot area, distances between parcels and the number of different crops appearing in the succession were calculated with R. [SOURCE: Page_127]

### 3.3.2. On-farm surveys

In order to know the logic behind the temporary plot exchanges, we conducted surveys on the farms involved in plot exchanges to understand the individual and collective [SOURCE: Page_127]

logics (both agronomic and sociological) that govern the exchange modalities between farmers. [SOURCE: Page_127]

The approach consisted on targeting the farms in the databases and then contacting them for a semi-directed interview. We made contact with the first farmers through the [SOURCE: Page_127]

catchment managers, and then, through the so-called snowball procedure, we contacted other farmers who were suggested to us by previous respondents. This [SOURCE: Page_127]

approach resulted in a sample of 16 farmers interviewed out of a total of 51 farms identified as carrying out temporary plot exchanges in the Escrebieux catchment area [SOURCE: Page_127]

(the list of farmers interviewed is presented in the appendix). [SOURCE: Page_127]

The survey was constructed and carried out according to the principles and techniques for analysing the overall functioning of the farm described by Capillon and Manichon [SOURCE: Page_127]

(1991). The interview grid was structured to: [SOURCE: Page_127]

   Gather information about farm production (type, yields and agricultural [SOURCE: Page_127]

practices) and structure (number of employees, etc.).    Highlight the details of cropping systems according to the characteristics of the [SOURCE: Page_127]

plots, in particular the cropping plan and crop succession.    Understand the practice of temporary plot exchange, especially the reasons for [SOURCE: Page_127]

the practice (e.g. how do temporary plots exchanges work? What are the criteria for selecting candidate plots for exchange? What are the modalities of [SOURCE: Page_127]

temporary plot exchanges?) And the difficulties of implementing it. [SOURCE: Page_127]

129 [SOURCE: Page_127]

During the interview, the farm territory map of each farm printed in A$_4$ format was provided to the farmers to allow them to explain the logic of the farm territory [SOURCE: Page_128]

management and its consequences for the plot exchanges. [SOURCE: Page_128]

The interviews lasted 1–2 hours, depending on the availability of the farmer and the [SOURCE: Page_128]

complexity of their operations. [SOURCE: Page_128]

To analyse data from surveys, we proceeded as follows: each farm in the survey was first characterised according to its structure (from the UAA, dispersion of plots, [SOURCE: Page_128]

equipment), cropping plan and agronomic constraints, the type (i.e. bilateral or one-side transfer) and frequency of plot exchange carried out, and the farmers’ [SOURCE: Page_128]

justifications for participating in temporary plot exchanges. We then grouped similar reasons for the exchanges and associated them with the characteristics of [SOURCE: Page_128]

the corresponding farms. In this way, we were able to establish a profile of the exchanging farms according to the nature and frequency of their need to exchange. [SOURCE: Page_128]

The survey helped construct an understanding of the potential interests of farmers [SOURCE: Page_128]

when exchanging plots, the financial arrangements that might be in place and how [SOURCE: Page_128]

the relationship between the exchanging farmers is maintained. [SOURCE: Page_128]

### 4.1.        Dynamics of plot exchanges in the Escrebieux site

We present here the exchanges that took place between the farms in the catchment [SOURCE: Page_129]

area but also between those inside and outside the area. Of the 146 farms in the Escrebieux catchment area, 51 had carried out at least one temporary plot exchange [SOURCE: Page_129]

(marked by the presence of a boomerang plot) over the 2015-2019 period. Over this period, an average of 553.85 ha of boomerang plots moved from one farm to another [SOURCE: Page_129]

each year. This represents approximately 9% of the UAA of the Escrebieux catchment area. The surface area of the exchanged plots varied from 0.1 to 20 ha with an average [SOURCE: Page_129]

of 2.875 ha which is close to the average size of all LPIS plots in the Escrebieux site, estimated at 3.15 ha. [SOURCE: Page_129]

### 4.1.1. Diversity of plot exchanges according to duration

There were several forms of temporary plot exchanges in the Escrebieux catchment [SOURCE: Page_129]

area that differed in duration. The durations of the exchanges varied between 1 year and 3 years or more (we were not able to examine boomerang plots beyond 3 years [SOURCE: Page_129]

with our 5-year data). The yearly exchanges represent 80% of the total exchanges in the territory. The results for the diversity of plot exchanges are summarised in Table 7. [SOURCE: Page_129]

### 4.1.2. Typology of plot exchanges in the Escrebieux catchment area

The observation of plot transfers between farms, taken in pairs, has made it possible to establish a typology of exchanges based on whether or not the exchange was [SOURCE: Page_130]

reciprocal. Three types of temporary plot exchanges can be distinguished. [SOURCE: Page_130]

   Type 1: Bilateral plot transfer. Two farms transfer parcels to each other in the [SOURCE: Page_130]

same year. Farm A transfers a parcel to Farm B, which in turn transfers a parcel to Farm A in the same year. This type of exchange makes up 75% of the [SOURCE: Page_130]

exchanges observed in the Escrebieux catchment area during the study period.    Type 2: One-sided plot transfer. A farm transfers one or more plots to another [SOURCE: Page_130]

farm in a given year without ever receiving a plot in return. Approximately 20% of the exchanges observed during the study period were one-sided. [SOURCE: Page_130]

   Type 3: Bilateral but deferred plot transfer. Two farms transfer plots to each other but in different years. Farm A transfers a parcel to Farm B and only receives [SOURCE: Page_130]

it back from Farm B in the year following the first transfer. Type 3 is relatively rare in the Escrebieux area, representing only 5% of the exchanges observed [SOURCE: Page_130]

during the study period. [SOURCE: Page_130]

### 4.1.3. Clusters of farms for plot exchanges

We identified farm clusters that exchanged plots with each other. These transfers can [SOURCE: Page_131]

be one-sided (one farm transfers a plot to another farm without reciprocity) or bilateral (two farms transfer plots to each other). We called the farm with the most exchanges [SOURCE: Page_131]

of plots the hub farm. ‘Partner farms’ are farms that exchange plots with the hub farm (Martin et al. 2018) (See Figure 15). [SOURCE: Page_131]

At the level of the Escrebieux water catchment area, we identified 13 independent ‘clusters’ distributed as follows: 7 clusters of 2 farms each; 2 clusters of 3 farms each; 2 [SOURCE: Page_131]

clusters of 4 farms each, including 1 hub farm; 1 cluster of 8 farms, including 1 hub farm; and 1 cluster of 17 farms, including 1 hub farm. Must Hub farms of the identified [SOURCE: Page_131]

clusters are potato producer (see Appendix 4 of this manuscript). [SOURCE: Page_131]

### 4.1.4. Annual exchange crops

The crop of the exchange corresponds to the crop grown on the boomerang plot during its year of departure/absence or presence from the farm territory. Figure 16 [SOURCE: Page_131]

presents all the crops found for the annual exchanges of the Escrebieux catchment area between 2015 and 2019. Among these crops, three were predominant: potatoes, sugar beets and soft winter wheat. Within the study period, approximately 40% of the [SOURCE: Page_131]

133 [SOURCE: Page_131]

boomerang plots area was cropped with potatoes; while it’s cropping surface occupies only 12% of the total utilized agricultural area of the Escrebieux catchment area. This [SOURCE: Page_132]

suggests that exchanges in the Escrebieux catchment area are primarily dictated by the need for surface area for potato production [SOURCE: Page_132]

### 4.2.          Socio-technical reasons of inter-farm temporary plot exchanges

We identified several reasons why farmers make temporary plot exchanges, which fall into two categories: agronomic and social reasons. [SOURCE: Page_132]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

### 4.2.1. Agronomic reasons

4.2.1.1.      The need for diversified crops in successions Based on the crop sequence data, we estimated the number of crops in crop [SOURCE: Page_132]

successions between 2015 and 2019 in the Escrebieux catchment area by differentiating between boomerang and non-boomerang plots. The results of this [SOURCE: Page_132]

calculation are presented in Table 8. [SOURCE: Page_132]

134 [SOURCE: Page_132]

Table 8: crop diversity in crop successions between boomerang and non-boomerang plots in the Escrebieux catchment area. The average of the crops in the succession is the sum of the crops of all crop successions over the total of the crop successions. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Number of crops in the           Boomerang plots                  Non-boomerang plots succession Proportion of total (%) [SOURCE: Page_133]

1                 9.87                                             13.09 [SOURCE: Page_133]

2                14.03                                             15.69 [SOURCE: Page_133]

3                44.16                                             42.68 [SOURCE: Page_133]

4                29.52                                             24.74 [SOURCE: Page_133]

5                 2.42                                              3.79 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Average number of crops in the                   3.01                                              2.91 [SOURCE: Page_133]

succession [SOURCE: Page_133]

The results of the student t-test are as follows: t = 58.159, df = 1, p-value = 0.01095. The p-value of the test is less than 0.05, meaning that the number of crops in [SOURCE: Page_133]

boomerang plots successions was different from that in non-boomerang plots. This further implies that the temporary exchange of plots makes it possible to move towards [SOURCE: Page_133]

greater diversification of successions within a standard of crop diversity acceptable to farmers according to local agricultural standards. [SOURCE: Page_133]

The diversification of crops through temporary plot exchanges is both quantitative and [SOURCE: Page_133]

qualitative. Quantitative diversification refers to the number of crops in the succession as mentioned in table 9. Qualitative diversification refers to the agronomic function [SOURCE: Page_133]

(e.g. reduction of the threat of parasites or breaking the weed cycle) of the different botanical species present in the succession. The farm interviews highlighted that for [SOURCE: Page_133]

cereal producers, temporary plot exchange makes it possible to introduce a break crop (i.e. a plant that will help to improve the structure of the soil through its roots and that [SOURCE: Page_133]

will help to reduce weeds for the following crops) into the succession. According to cereal famers interviewed, to diversify the families of crops in the rotation and avoid a [SOURCE: Page_133]

135 [SOURCE: Page_133]

succession of the same crop family several years in a row on the same plots, they resort to temporary plot exchanges with other farmers oriented towards different types of [SOURCE: Page_134]

production (e.g. industrial crops such as potatoes). One cereal farmer interviewed declare: “The break crop introduced by the exchange plots “acts as a weed killer for the [SOURCE: Page_134]

cereals and improves the soil structure” to explain its motivation to resort to temporary plot exchanges. [SOURCE: Page_134]

4.2.1.2. The need to reduce the stress constraints on crop return time of certain crops Surveys have shown that long return times are a constraint for certain crops and favour the recourse to temporary plot exchanges. According to the interviews, some potato [SOURCE: Page_134]

farms may face saturation regarding the potato return time and release this tension through year-round exchanges of plots. For example, Farmer A146 (see table in [SOURCE: Page_134]

appendix) explained in his interview that he has an annual potato production capacity of 30 ha on his farm (available crop area considering the potato return period of 4 years [SOURCE: Page_134]

in this area). However, his annual target for potatoes is 60 ha, resulting in a deficit of 30 ha of land he must find each year to reach his objectives. To compensate for this [SOURCE: Page_134]

shortfall, he outsources part of his cropping plan through temporary plot exchanges. If he does not manage to obtain the plots through exchanges, he must cut back on the [SOURCE: Page_134]

time needed to return the potatoes, facing consequences from the application of pesticides that this requires [explanation given by Farmer A146 interviewed on [SOURCE: Page_134]

10/01/2021]. Similar reasoning was given by other farmers in the survey, notably the potato growers. [SOURCE: Page_134]

An extreme case of stress on the return time of crops was noted for farms hyper- [SOURCE: Page_134]

specialised in potatoes, as their farmers have invested in equipment (e.g. tractor, [SOURCE: Page_134]

sowing machine, and harvesters) or storage sheds for the crops to produce larger quantities of potatoes. For these farms, the return time constraint is linked to the high [SOURCE: Page_134]

surface area requirements of potatoes in the cropping plan. The aim to produce large areas of potatoes forces farmers to resort to either exchange or the provision of plots, generally by subletting. For example, Farmer A126, a potato producer, invested in a [SOURCE: Page_134]

136 [SOURCE: Page_134]

storage shed with a storage capacity of 5,000 tonnes (corresponding to 120 ha of potatoes if the yield is 40-45 tonnes/ha). To make this investment profitable, he needs [SOURCE: Page_135]

to produce 120 ha of potatoes each year. On his farm, he can only produce 25 ha of potatoes each year due to the available cultivation area. Furthermore, he has limited [SOURCE: Page_135]

possibilities to expand his farm due to a lack of land available for purchase in this area. Thus, to reach his production targets, he needs to acquire an additional 95 ha from [SOURCE: Page_135]

other farmers. If he does not manage to do so through exchanges or renting, he is forced to reduce his potato area. He would therefore be forced to pay twice as much [SOURCE: Page_135]

for harvesting and planting. In the long run, he would be forced to stop his activity. [SOURCE: Page_135]

“As we find it difficult to expand to increase our income, and as expansion [SOURCE: Page_135]

opportunities are rare, subletting land – even if it is not sustainable over time (because the farmer may decide to stop subletting to you) – or exchange of plot [SOURCE: Page_135]

temporarily allows us to develop income on the farm”. [Explanation given by Farmer A126 interviewed on 12/01/2020] [SOURCE: Page_135]

4.2.1.3. The need to reduce constraints linked to the previous crop when allocating land to the crop The possibility of producing one crop on a parcel is not only reliant on the return period [SOURCE: Page_135]

of the crop but also the cultivation history of the parcel with a particular emphasis on the previous crop. The allocation of land to the crops inside the farm landscape in the [SOURCE: Page_135]

current year is also determined by the previous year’s crop. A farmer may find himself in a situation where poor harvesting conditions of the previous crop reduce the [SOURCE: Page_135]

possibility of establishing the desired crop on the plot in question. In this situation, a temporary plot exchange seems to circumvent this constraint. Farmer A$_8$ explained that [SOURCE: Page_135]

he wanted to grow potatoes on plots previously used for grain maize. Due to rainy [SOURCE: Page_135]

weather, the maize harvest left ruts on the plots, degrading the soil structure which are unfavourable conditions for potato planting. Because of this constraint, he resorted to [SOURCE: Page_135]

exchanging plots temporarily with a farm that wanted to produce wheat to be able to plant his potatoes [summary of interview of Farmer A$_8$ interviewed on 10/12/2020]. [SOURCE: Page_135]

137 [SOURCE: Page_135]

### 4.2.2. Social reasons of farms

Some farmers exchange plots for reasons other than need. The interviews revealed that [SOURCE: Page_136]

some plot exchanges are motivated by the social proximity farmers have with other farmers. This proximity can be professional or family related. For example, Farmer A88 [SOURCE: Page_136]

is a mixed crop and livestock farmer with a focus on sugar beets. He has no need to exchange a priori, but he does so with his cousin – a potato producer who needs more [SOURCE: Page_136]

land. He agrees to exchange plots to relieve the tension on his cousin's farm and considers the plot exchange a service he provides for a family member [summary of [SOURCE: Page_136]

interview with Farmer A88, interviewed on 02/02/2021]. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Plot exchange is also done for service needs. Two farmers from the survey who do not [SOURCE: Page_136]

wish to renew their equipment use this method. The exchange is generally done with potato producers in search of land for their potatoes. The exchange may be [SOURCE: Page_136]

compensated by the potato producer carrying out part of the cultivation operations (e.g. ploughing, sowing, harvesting). A trusting relationship is thus built between the [SOURCE: Page_136]

co-exchangers. In some cases, the trust goes beyond the perimeter of the plot exchange and leads to a strategy of land acquisition. Specifically, the field surveys [SOURCE: Page_136]

revealed that some potato producers take over the plots of cereal farmers when they are close to retirement. [SOURCE: Page_136]

### 4.3.    Classification of farms according to the frequency of need for temporary plot exchanges

The reasons of the exchanges described above, based on database analysis and [SOURCE: Page_136]

interviews, enable the classification of the farms according to the frequency of need for temporary plot exchanges. Two types of plot exchange situations emerged: [SOURCE: Page_136]

recurrent and occasional needs for plot exchange. These differ in terms of the [SOURCE: Page_136]

motivations behind the exchanges, the production orientation and the type of exchange carried out. Table 9 summarises these two types of plot exchanges situations. [SOURCE: Page_136]

The need to exchange is recurrent for hyper-specialised farms that carry out bilateral and unilateral transfers to ensure that the investments made in potato cultivation are [SOURCE: Page_136]

138 [SOURCE: Page_136]

profitable. In contrast, the need is occasional on farms that face either specific agronomic constraints linked to the return period or the previous crop of the plot [SOURCE: Page_137]

(these farms generally resort to bilateral plot transfers) or the social constraints of helping out a colleague or friend through plot exchange. [SOURCE: Page_137]

4.4.      Beyond the socio-technical reasons, the landscape characteristics for effective temporary plot exchanges between farms [SOURCE: Page_139]

Here we describe the main landscape features favourable to the development of plot exchanges between farms in the Escrebieux catchment area. [SOURCE: Page_139]

### 4.4.1. Geographical proximity of the farms

Observation of the spatial organisation of farm territories in the catchment area [SOURCE: Page_139]

revealed a certain geographical proximity between the exchanging farms. The distribution of the distances between the plots with each other in the Escrebieux [SOURCE: Page_139]

catchment area, represented by boxplots in Figure 17, shows that the plots of the farms involved in the plot exchange were relatively close to each other with an average of [SOURCE: Page_139]

3.5km. The greatest distance between plots of farms that exchanged plots (calculated based on the distance matrix between plots) was approximately 10 km. This [SOURCE: Page_139]

information indicates the importance of distance in the practice of temporary plot exchange. [SOURCE: Page_139]

One of the reasons for this geographical proximity between the plots of the exchanging [SOURCE: Page_139]

farms is the logistical constraints. According to the explanation given by 4 interviewed farmers, geographical proximity allows the deployment of an irrigation system on the [SOURCE: Page_139]

neighbouring plot if one is not already in place or facilitating the export of wheat straw. [SOURCE: Page_139]

### 4.4.2. Trust between farmers

Trust in plot exchanges is the assurance that one’s plot will be treated well when it is temporarily exchanged with another farmer. The farmer surveys highlighted the [SOURCE: Page_141]

importance of trust between the parties involved in the exchange. The farms that exchange plots generally belong to circles of friends or networks where trust has been [SOURCE: Page_141]

built up over time, and it is based on this trust that they exchange plots. The bond of trust between farmers is generally born of their proximity throughout their lives: [SOURCE: Page_141]

“I trade with a farmer who was my biggest customer when I was a salesman for a distributor of agricultural inputs. When he asked me to exchange plot, I didn't dare [SOURCE: Page_141]

refuse him because he is a good customer and we have known each other for a long time, and we trust each other”. [Explanation given by Farmer A53 interviewed [SOURCE: Page_141]

on 08/02/2021 on his farm located in the catchment area] [SOURCE: Page_141]

Neighbourhoods can, in some cases, facilitate the building of trust. [SOURCE: Page_141]

“The farmers I deal with are my neighbours, and we have known each other for a [SOURCE: Page_141]

very long time. This relationship of trust is essential”. [Explanation given by Farmer A124 interviewed on 07/02/2021] [SOURCE: Page_141]

### 5.1.     New methodology to address the dynamics of farm cropping plans over short periods

This study of temporary plot exchanges and their socio-technical reasons highlights [SOURCE: Page_142]

the value of crossing quantitative data (here, LPIS data) and qualitative data (farm-level surveys) to address the dynamics of farm cropping plans, over short periods (Schaller [SOURCE: Page_142]

2011; Bouty 2015; Barbottin et al. 2018). By combining these two information sources, the advantages of each could be exploited while overcoming their limitations. In our [SOURCE: Page_142]

study, the LPIS data allowed us to collect information on a large scale (Steinmann and Dobers 2013a): we were able to characterise all the farms present in the Escrebieux [SOURCE: Page_142]

catchment area in terms of their plot exchange practices. The comprehensive field study, on the other hand, allowed us to gain a more detailed understanding of the [SOURCE: Page_142]

mechanisms and socio-technical reasons of plot exchanges, which are not visible only with the LPIS data. In short, the combination of the two approaches made it possible [SOURCE: Page_142]

to more widely test what can be learned from surveys on a relatively small sample (in this case, information on the proximity of the plots that are exchanged) and compare [SOURCE: Page_142]

different sources of information to draw conclusions. [SOURCE: Page_142]

This methodological approach also enabled us to define several spatial scales – the plot, the farm and the landscape– and iterate between them (Debolini 2021). The plot scale allowed us to identify the crops for which exchanges take place and determine [SOURCE: Page_142]

the volume of plot exchanges per year. The scale of the farm made it possible to understand the reasons of the exchanges and the farmers' logics with regard to the [SOURCE: Page_142]

exchanges of plots. The territorial scale allowed us to examine the conditions favourable to the development of exchanges. An extension of our approach to other [SOURCE: Page_142]

territories where there is a tradition of temporary exchanges of plots between farmers would be an interesting perspective to explore. For example, the approach could be [SOURCE: Page_142]

used in the Argentinean Pampa to characterise the socio-technical reasons of potato farms that carry out temporary plot exchanges (Salembier et al.2016). However, the [SOURCE: Page_142]

144 [SOURCE: Page_142]

application of the approach will only be possible if the LPIS data or similar data are [SOURCE: Page_143]

available. [SOURCE: Page_143]

5.2.     Temporary plot exchanges as a coordinating approach to allocation of crops between different farmers [SOURCE: Page_143]

Crop allocation refers to the spatial distribution of cropping plans at the farm level [SOURCE: Page_143]

Ronfort et al. 2011; Dury et al. 2011; Peltonen-Sainio et al. 2019). In the literature, agronomic work on crop allocation decisions has generally considered allocating fields [SOURCE: Page_143]

for different crops as a decision to be made by individual farms ( El-Nazer and McCarl 1986; Maxime et al. 1995; Aubry et al. 1998; Dury et al. 2013). However, these studies [SOURCE: Page_143]

did not consider the possible interactions between farmers when allocating crops to plots. Our study shows that a certain level of crop allocation can be coordinated [SOURCE: Page_143]

between several farms. Plot exchange is used when a farmer is unable to allocate the entire area desired for certain crops on his plots due to constraints such as the return [SOURCE: Page_143]

period of the crop. A farmer who resorts to temporary plot exchange is highly dependent on the crop allocation of his co-exchanger, as the plot to be exchanged is [SOURCE: Page_143]

identified through necessary adjustments to the cropping plan objectives (Haneveld and Stegeman 2005; Le Bail et al. 2006) of the co-exchanger. The allocation of all the [SOURCE: Page_143]

current year's crops is only possible when the farm manages to outsource part of its cropping plan to another farmer. Crop allocation is thus coordinated between different [SOURCE: Page_143]

farmers through plot exchanges. [SOURCE: Page_143]

The strategy of crop allocation through temporary plot strategy for potato farmers of [SOURCE: Page_143]

Escrebieux catchment area can be summarised in five main steps as presented in Figure 18 below. At the beginning of the season, the farmer defines his cropping plan i.e. area [SOURCE: Page_143]

of each crop (step 1). Then, he evaluates his plots for allocation of crop (step 2). This evaluation is based on the history of crop successions on the plots, which makes it [SOURCE: Page_143]

possible to estimate the suitability of each plot to receive a crop. The farmer then proceeds to choose the plots for each crop (step 3). He starts with the crop he considers to be the main crop for his farm (i.e. the crop for which the farmer seeks to optimise [SOURCE: Page_143]

145 [SOURCE: Page_143]

production [Schaller 2011]). There are two situations: either the farmer manages to [SOURCE: Page_144]

allocate the entire area of his main crop to the selected plots, in which case his crop rotation target is reached, or he does not manage to do so. When the cropping plan [SOURCE: Page_144]

objective is not achieved, the farmer resorts to temporary plot exchange (step 4). When a farm is in need of exchange, it "looks around" for plots of land adjacent to its own [SOURCE: Page_144]

and belonging to farms with which there is a relationship of trust. If these plots meet the agronomic criteria of the crop precedent and the return period of the main crop, [SOURCE: Page_144]

then the requesting farm can initiate the request. Generally, plot exchanges only take [SOURCE: Page_144]

place between farms with different production systems. Once the plot exchange is completed, the farmer finalises his cropping plan (step 5). [SOURCE: Page_144]

In addition to crop allocation, temporary plot exchanges allow for crop diversification [SOURCE: Page_144]

at the level of successions (Boulestreau et al. 2021). The number of crops observed in the successions on the plots in the Escrebieux area is a result of this diversification [SOURCE: Page_144]

strategy. Thus, temporary plot exchanges respond to an agronomic logic of diversification of crop successions and lengthening of rotations. This is in line with [SOURCE: Page_144]

(Goffart et al. 2022) who recommend diversifying potato-based rotations and respect a 4- or 5-year rotation at least, to reduce the development of nematodes or other soil [SOURCE: Page_144]

related pests and diseases. [SOURCE: Page_144]

### 5.3.      Beyond the exchange: A land control strategy?

We observed that plot exchanges between farms also respond to land control strategies (Holtslag-Broekhof et al. 2014) in an area where access to land is made [SOURCE: Page_146]

difficult by strong competition between urban and agricultural areas. The relationship of trust built between the co-exchanging farms extends beyond the period of the [SOURCE: Page_146]

exchange and, in certain circumstances, enables one of the partners to be positioned [SOURCE: Page_146]

as a privileged actor when a plot is taken over. For example, an exchange between a potato farmer and a cereal farmer will generally be accompanied by a reward in service. [SOURCE: Page_146]

The potato producer will do the cereal farmer a favour by ploughing his plots for free. In this way, the act of production on the cereal farmer's plots is partially externalised (Anzalone and Purseigle 2014) and contributes to maintaining a long-term relationship of trust or even dependency. [SOURCE: Page_146]

This long-term construction of the social link between farms that exchange plots of [SOURCE: Page_146]

land is therefore a means of securing land by seizing takeover opportunities. As mentioned in the field surveys, some potato producers take over the plots of cereal [SOURCE: Page_146]

farmers when they are close to retirement. These potato producers, generally with a strong financial capacity simply deal directly with the landowners who are going out of [SOURCE: Page_146]

business, offering prices higher than market values and positioning themselves as preferred buyers. [SOURCE: Page_146]

This land control strategy (Holtslag-Broekhof et al. 2014) through temporary plot exchanges may give rise to new rules for the operation of temporary plot exchanges. [SOURCE: Page_146]

For example, the monetary or service compensation (ploughing of the field) currently required of the potato producer who requests the cereal farmer's plot could be [SOURCE: Page_146]

systematised. Another consequence is the potential loss of the territory's agricultural diversity, a corollary of the high concentration of land in the hands of the potato [SOURCE: Page_146]

producers to the detriment of other farms. [SOURCE: Page_146]

### 5.4.    Modelling perspectives to estimate the environmental benefit of temporary plot exchanges

This study provided generic methods and tools to understand the socio-technical [SOURCE: Page_147]

dimensions of temporary plot exchanges between farmers in a given landscape. It also [SOURCE: Page_147]

highlighted the agronomic indicators (e.g. diversity of successions, lengthening of return periods) that result from the exchange of plots and are known to be agronomic [SOURCE: Page_147]

levers for reducing the environmental pressure of agriculture ; Bockstaller and Girardin 2002; B. Leteinturier et al. 2006b; Colbach et al. 2010; Câmara-Salim et al. 2021). To [SOURCE: Page_147]

assess the environmental impact of the exchanges of plots, it seems appropriate to formalise the process of temporary plot exchange and the resulting agronomic [SOURCE: Page_147]

consequences, particularly in terms of crop rotation and succession. Modelling seems to be the best approach for simulating the conditions under which parcel exchanges [SOURCE: Page_147]

can lead to environmental gains (Bakker et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Developing models can help increase our understanding of the reasons of temporary plot exchanges to design spatial organisations of cropping systems that limit [SOURCE: Page_147]

agricultural pollution (Le Ber et al. 2006; Wochele et al. 2014). Models also enable simulation of scenarios and the evaluation of the outputs of the simulation. Those such [SOURCE: Page_147]

as LandSFACTS (developed by Castellazzi et al. 2010) allow for the generation of crop allocations at the landscape scale through various spatiotemporal scenarios of crop [SOURCE: Page_147]

arrangement. We plan to develop a model that closely follows this approach and can produce different types of indicators (e.g. crop rotation, succession) and simulate [SOURCE: Page_147]

cropping plan evolutions compatible with environmental protection (Leteinturier et al. 2006b). For example, it should be able to model the Functioning of temporary plot [SOURCE: Page_147]

exchanges as shown in Figure and propose scenarios such as the introduction of a diversification crop with low input consumption in the landscape but the same return [SOURCE: Page_147]

time constraints as potatoes (which are the main crop in the exchanges in Escrebieux) to determine the environmental gains of the exchanges when such a crop is introduced [SOURCE: Page_147]

at the landscape level. Future work could help formalise this model. [SOURCE: Page_147]

### 6. Conclusion

This work aimed to better understand the socio-technical reasons of plot exchanges [SOURCE: Page_148]

between farms from an agronomic point of view. The developed methodology allowed us to characterise the dynamics of temporary plot exchanges and understand their [SOURCE: Page_148]

socio-technical reasons at the scale of a small territory. Our results show that 51 farmers out of the 147 with at least one parcel in the Escrebieux catchment area have made [SOURCE: Page_148]

plot exchanges at least once between 2015 and 2019. We also showed that temporary plot exchanges are generally driven by crop allocation constraints and are part of a [SOURCE: Page_148]

reasoning strategy coordinated between several farmers to overcome this constraint. In terms of farm structure, plot exchanges led to frequent reconfigurations of farm [SOURCE: Page_148]

territory. Our results add to the work of Barbottin et al. (2018), who highlighted the reconfiguration of farm plots at short time intervals. [SOURCE: Page_148]

The general validity of the knowledge acquired on the mechanisms of exchanges and their socio-technical reasons from the analysis of a single site could be questioned. [SOURCE: Page_148]

However, the results of this work show that, from an agronomic point of view, plot exchanges widen farmers’ opportunities for allocating land to crops. [SOURCE: Page_148]

For the authorities in charge of managing drinking water catchment areas, the process of identifying temporary exchanges of plots in a territory developed in this paper can help to identify the farmland dynamics in their territory and to anticipate the [SOURCE: Page_148]

spatiotemporal organisation of the cropping systems that will result and thus better plan the implementation of agricultural practices compatible with the protection of the [SOURCE: Page_148]

water resource. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Our study contributes to landscape agronomy as it provides a new framework for [SOURCE: Page_148]

studying the spatiotemporal organisation of cropping systems and their link to the environment by observing changes in agricultural landscape over a short period. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Remy, who was very helpful during the farmers’ surveys. [SOURCE: Page_149]
